scientist and engineer</li>\n<li><a href="/wiki/Iron_Man%27s_armor" title="Iron Man&#39;s armor">Powered armor suit</a>:\n<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Superhuman_strength" title="Superhuman href="/wiki/Life_support" title="Life support">life support</a></li></ul></li></ul>\n</td></tr></tbody></table>\n<p><b>Iron Man</b> is a fictional <a href="/wiki/Superhero" title="Superhero">superhero</a> appearing in <a href="/wiki/American_comic_book" title="American comic book">American comic books</a> published by <a href="/wiki/Marvel_Comics" title="Marvel Comics">Marvel Comics</a>. The character was co-created by writer and editor <a href="/wiki/Stan_Lee" title="Stan Lee">Stan Lee</a>, developed by scripter <a href="/wiki/Larry_Lieber" title="Larry Lieber">Larry Lieber</a>, and designed by artists <a href="/wiki/Don_Heck" title="Don Heck">Don Heck</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jack_Kirby" title="Jack Kirby">Jack Kirby</a>. The character made his <a href="/wiki/First_appearance" title="First appearance">first appearance</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/Tales_of_Suspense" title="Tales of Suspense">Tales of Suspense</a></i> #39 (<a href="/wiki/Cover_date" title="Cover date">cover dated</a> March 1963), and received his own title in <i><a href="/wiki/Iron_Man_(comic_book)" title="Iron Man (comic book)">Iron Man</a></i> #1 (May 1968).\n</p><p>A wealthy American <a href="/wiki/Business_magnate" title="Business magnate">business magnate</a>, <a href="/wiki/Playboy_(lifestyle)" class="mw-redirect" title="Playboy (lifestyle)">playboy</a>, and ingenious <a href="/wiki/Scientist" title="Scientist">scientist</a>, <b>Anthony Edward</b> "<b>Tony</b>" <b>Stark</b> suffers a severe chest injury during a kidnapping. When his captors attempt to force him to build a <a href="/wiki/Weapon_of_mass_destruction" title="Weapon of mass destruction">weapon of mass destruction</a>, he instead creates a <a href="/wiki/Iron_Man%27s_armor" title="Iron Man&#39;s armor">mechanized suit of armor</a> to save his life and escape captivity. Later, Stark develops his suit, adding weapons and other technological devices he designed through his company, <a href="/wiki/Stark_Industries" title="Stark Industries">Stark Industries</a>. He uses the suit and successive versions to protect the world as Iron Man. Although at first concealing his true identity, Stark eventually declared that he was, in fact, Iron Man in a public announcement.\n</p><p>Initially, Iron Man was a vehicle for Stan Lee to explore <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> themes, particularly the role of American technology and industry in the fight against <a href="/wiki/Communism" title="Communism">communism</a>. Subsequent re-imaginings of Iron Man have transitioned from Cold War motifs to contemporary matters of the time.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>\n</p><p>Throughout most of the character\'s publication history, Iron Man has been a founding member of the superhero team the <a href="/wiki/Avengers_(comics)" title="Avengers (comics)">Avengers</a> and has been featured in several incarnations of his own various comic book series. Iron Man has been adapted for several animated TV shows and films. The <a href="/wiki/Tony_Stark_(Marvel_Cinematic_Universe)" title="Tony Stark (Marvel Cinematic Universe)">Marvel Cinematic Universe character</a> was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Downey_Jr." title="Robert Downey Jr.">Robert Downey Jr.</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Marvel_Cinematic_Universe" title="Marvel Cinematic Universe">Marvel Cinematic Universe</a> films <i><a href="/wiki/Iron_Man_(2008_film)" title="Iron Man (2008 film)">Iron Man</a></i> (2008), <i><a href="/wiki/The_Incredible_Hulk_(film)" title="The Incredible Hulk (film)">The Incredible Hulk</a></i> (2008) in a cameo, <i><a href="/wiki/Iron_Man_2" title="Iron Man 2">Iron Man 2</a></i> (2010), <i><a href="/wiki/The_Avengers_(2012_film)" title="The Avengers (2012 film)">The Avengers</a></i> (2012), <i><a href="/wiki/Iron_Man_3" title="Iron Man 3">Iron Man 3</a></i> (2013), <i><a href="/wiki/Avengers:_Age_of_Ultron" title="Avengers: Age of Ultron">Avengers: Age of Ultron</a></i> (2015), <i><a href="/wiki/Captain_America:_Civil_War" title="Captain America: Civil War">Captain America: Civil War</a></i> (2016), <i><a href="/wiki/Spider-Man:_Homecoming" title="Spider-Man: Homecoming">Spider-Man: Homecoming</a></i> (2017), <i><a href="/wiki/Avengers:_Infinity_War" title="Avengers: Infinity War">Avengers: Infinity War</a></i> (2018), <i><a href="/wiki/Avengers:_Endgame" title="Avengers: Endgame">Avengers: Endgame</a></i> (2019), and will reprise his role in <i><a href="/wiki/Black_Widow_(2020_film)" title="Black Widow (2020 film)">Black Widow</a></i> (2020).\n</p><p>Iron Man was ranked 12th on <a href="/wiki/IGN" title="IGN">IGN</a>\'s "Top 100 Comic Book Heroes" in 2011<sup id="cite_ref-IGNTop100_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IGNTop100-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> and third in their list of "The Top 50 Avengers" in 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>\n</p>\n<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r886046785">.mw-parser-output .toclimit-2 .toclevel-1 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-3 .toclevel-2 class="tocnumber">6</span> <span class="toctext">In other media</span></a></li>\n<li class="toclevel-1 tocsection-31"><a href="#Cultural_influence_and_legacy"><span class="tocnumber">7</span> <span class="toctext">Cultural influence and legacy</span></a></li>\n<li class="toclevel-1 tocsection-32"><a href="#See_also"><span class="tocnumber">8</span> <span class="toctext">See also</span></a></li>\n<li class="toclevel-1 tocsection-33"><a href="#References"><span class="tocnumber">9</span> <span class="toctext">References</span></a></li>\n<li class="toclevel-1 tocsection-34"><a href="#Further_reading"><span class="tocnumber">10</span> <span class="toctext">Further reading</span></a></li>\n<li class="toclevel-1 tocsection-35"><a href="#External_links"><span class="tocnumber">11</span> <span class="toctext">External links</span></a></li>\n</ul>\n</div>\n</div>\n<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Publication_history">Publication history</span></h2>\n<div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/List_of_Iron_Man_titles" title="List of Iron Man titles">List of Iron Man titles</a></div>\n<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Premiere">Premiere</span></h3>\n<p>Iron Man\'s <a href="/wiki/Marvel_Comics" title="Marvel Comics">Marvel Comics</a> premiere in <i>Tales of Suspense</i> #39 (<a href="/wiki/Cover_date" title="Cover date">cover dated</a> March 1963) was a collaboration among editor and story-plotter <a href="/wiki/Stan_Lee" title="Stan Lee">Stan Lee</a>, scripter <a href="/wiki/Larry_Lieber" title="Larry Lieber">Larry Lieber</a>, story-artist <a href="/wiki/Don_Heck" title="Don Heck">Don Heck</a>, and cover-artist and character-designer <a href="/wiki/Jack_Kirby" title="Jack Kirby">Jack Kirby</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> In 1963, Lee had been toying with the idea of a businessman superhero.<sup id="cite_ref-origins_45_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-origins_45-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> He wanted to create the "quintessential capitalist", a character that would go against the spirit of the times and Marvel\'s readership.<sup id="cite_ref-Excelsior_160_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Excelsior_160-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Lee said,\n</p>\n<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r886047036">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I think I gave myself a dare. It was the height of the <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a>. The readers, the young readers, if there was one thing they hated, it was war, it was the military ... So I got a hero who represented that to the hundredth degree. He was a weapons manufacturer, he was providing weapons for the Army, he was rich, he was an industrialist ... I thought it would be fun to take the kind of character that nobody would like, none of our readers would like, and shove him down their throats and make them like him ... And he became very popular.<sup id="cite_ref-IMDVD_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMDVD-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>\n</p></blockquote>\n<p>He set out to make the new character a wealthy, glamorous ladies\' man, but one with a secret that would plague and torment him as well.<sup id="cite_ref-origins_46_pt._1_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-origins_46_pt._1-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Writer <a href="/wiki/Gerry_Conway" title="Gerry Conway">Gerry Conway</a> said, "Here you have this character, who on the outside is invulnerable, I mean, just can\'t be touched, but inside is a wounded figure. Stan made it very much an in-your-face wound, you know, his heart was broken, you know, literally broken. But there\'s a metaphor going on there. And that\'s, I think, what made that character interesting."<sup id="cite_ref-IMDVD_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMDVD-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Lee based this playboy\'s looks and personality on <a href="/wiki/Howard_Hughes" title="Howard Hughes">Howard Hughes</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Maskarticle_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Maskarticle-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> explaining, "Howard Hughes was one of the most colorful men of our time. He was an inventor, an adventurer, a multi-billionaire, a ladies\' man and finally a nutcase."<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> "Without being crazy, he was Howard Hughes," Lee said.<sup id="cite_ref-IMDVD_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMDVD-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>\n</p><p>While Lee intended to write the story himself,<sup id="cite_ref-origins_46_pt._2_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-origins_46_pt._2-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> a minor deadline emergency eventually forced him to hand over the premiere issue to Lieber, who fleshed out the story.<sup id="cite_ref-origins_46_pt._1_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-origins_46_pt._1-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> The art was split between Kirby and Heck. "He designed the costume," Heck said of Kirby, "because he was doing the cover. The covers were always done first. But I created the look of the characters, like Tony Stark and his secretary <a href="/wiki/Pepper_Potts" title="Pepper Potts">Pepper Potts</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> In a 1990 interview, when asked if he had "a specific model for Tony Stark and the other characters?", Heck replied "No, I would be thinking more along the lines of some characters I like, which would be the same kind of characters that <a href="/wiki/Alex_Toth" title="Alex Toth">Alex Toth</a> liked, which was an <a href="/wiki/Errol_Flynn" title="Errol Flynn">Errol Flynn</a> type."<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Iron Man first appeared in 13- to 18-page stories in <i>Tales of Suspense,</i> which featured <a href="/wiki/Anthology" title="Anthology">anthology</a> <a href="/wiki/Science_fiction" title="Science fiction">science fiction</a> and <a href="/wiki/Supernatural" title="Supernatural">supernatural</a> stories. The character\'s original costume was a bulky gray armored suit, replaced by a golden version in the second story (issue #40, April 1963). It was redesigned as sleeker, red-and-golden armor in issue #48 (Dec. 1963) by that issue\'s interior artist, <a href="/wiki/Steve_Ditko" title="Steve Ditko">Steve Ditko</a>, although Kirby drew it on the cover. As Heck recalled in 1985, "[T]he second costume, the red and yellow one, was designed by Steve Ditko. I found it easier than drawing that bulky old thing. The earlier design, the robot-looking one, was more Kirbyish."<sup id="cite_ref-featurep18_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-featurep18-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>\n</p><p>In his premiere, Iron Man was an <a href="/wiki/Anti-communism" title="Anti-communism">anti-communist</a> hero, defeating various Vietnamese agents. Lee later regretted this early focus.<sup id="cite_ref-origins_45_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-origins_45-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Nation_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nation-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Throughout the character\'s <a href="/wiki/Comic_book" title="Comic book">comic book</a> series, technological advancement and <a href="/wiki/National_security" title="National security">national defense</a> were constant themes for Iron Man, but later issues developed Stark into a more complex and vulnerable character as they depicted his battle with <a href="/wiki/Alcoholism" title="Alcoholism">alcoholism</a> (as in the "<a href="/wiki/Demon_in_a_Bottle" title="Demon in a Bottle">Demon in a Bottle</a>" storyline) and other personal difficulties.\n</p><p>From issue #59 (Nov. 1964) to its final issue #99 (March 1968), the anthological <a href="/wiki/Science-fiction" class="mw-redirect" title="Science-fiction">science-fiction</a> backup stories in <i>Tales of Suspense</i> were replaced by a feature starring the superhero <a href="/wiki/Captain_America" title="Captain America">Captain America</a>. Lee and Heck introduced several adversaries for the character including the <a href="/wiki/Mandarin_(comics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mandarin (comics)">Mandarin</a> in issue #50 (Feb. 1964),<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Black_Widow_(Natasha_Romanova)" title="Black Widow (Natasha Romanova)">Black Widow</a> in #52 (April 1964)<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Hawkeye_(Clint_Barton)" title="Hawkeye (Clint Barton)">Hawkeye</a> five issues later.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>\n</p><p>Lee said that "of all the comic books we published at Marvel, we got more fan mail for Iron Man from women, from females, than any other title ... We didn\'t get much fan mail from girls, but whenever we did, the letter was usually addressed to Iron Man."<sup id="cite_ref-IMDVD_7-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IMDVD-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>\n</p><p>Lee and Kirby included Iron Man in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Avengers_(comic_book)" title="The Avengers (comic book)">The Avengers</a></i> #1 (Sept. 1963) as a founding member of the superhero team. The character has since appeared in every subsequent volume of the series.\n</p><p>Writers have updated the war and locale in which Stark is injured. In the original 1963 story, it was the <a href="/wiki/Vietnam_War" title="Vietnam War">Vietnam War</a>. In the 1990s, it was updated to be the first <a href="/wiki/Gulf_War" title="Gulf War">Gulf War</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-emerging_pop_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-emerging_pop-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> and in the 2000s updated again to be the <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%93present)" title="War in Afghanistan (2001\xe2\x80\x93present)">war in Afghanistan</a>. Stark\'s time with the Asian <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Prize" title="Nobel Prize">Nobel Prize</a>-winning scientist <a href="/wiki/Ho_Yinsen" title="Ho Yinsen">Ho Yinsen</a> is consistent through nearly all incarnations of the Iron Man origin, depicting Stark and Yinsen building the original armor together. One exception is the <a href="/wiki/Direct-to-DVD" class="mw-redirect" title="Direct-to-DVD">direct-to-DVD</a> animated feature film <i><a href="/wiki/The_Invincible_Iron_Man_(film)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Invincible Iron Man (film)">The Invincible Iron Man</a></i>, in which the armor Stark uses to escape his captors is not the first Iron Man suit.\n</p>\n<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Themes">Themes</span></h4>\n<p>The original <i>Iron Man</i> title explored <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a> themes, as did other <a href="/wiki/Stan_Lee" title="Stan Lee">Stan Lee</a> projects in the early years of <a href="/wiki/Marvel_Comics" title="Marvel Comics">Marvel Comics</a>. Where <i><a href="/wiki/The_Fantastic_Four" class="mw-redirect" title="The Fantastic Four">The Fantastic Four</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/The_Incredible_Hulk_(comic_book)" title="The Incredible Hulk (comic book)">The Incredible Hulk</a></i> respectively focused on American domestic and government responses to the Communist threat, <i>Iron Man</i> explored industry\'s role in the struggle. Tony Stark\'s real-life model, <a href="/wiki/Howard_Hughes" title="Howard Hughes">Howard Hughes</a>, was a significant defense contractor who developed new weapons technologies. Hughes was an icon both of American individualism and of the burdens of fame.<sup id="cite_ref-JournalofPopCulture_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JournalofPopCulture-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>\n</p><p>Historian Robert Genter, in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Journal_of_Popular_Culture" title="The Journal of Popular Culture">The Journal of Popular Culture</a></i>, writes that Tony Stark specifically presents an idealized portrait of the American inventor. Where earlier decades had seen important technological innovations come from famous <i>individuals</i> (e.g., <a href="/wiki/Nikola_Tesla" title="Nikola Tesla">Nikola Tesla</a>, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Edison" title="Thomas Edison">Thomas Edison</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Graham_Bell" title="Alexander Graham Bell">Alexander Graham Bell</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Wright_brothers" title="Wright brothers">Wright brothers</a>), the 1960s saw new technologies (including weapons) being developed mainly by the research teams of <i>corporations</i>. As a result, little room remained for the inventor who wanted credit for, and creative and economic control over, his/her own creations.\n</p><p>Issues of entrepreneurial autonomy, government supervision of research, and ultimate loyalty figured prominently in early <i>Iron Man</i> stories\xe2\x80\x94the same issues affecting American scientists and engineers of that era.<sup id="cite_ref-JournalofPopCulture_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JournalofPopCulture-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Tony Stark, writes Genter, is an inventor who finds motive in his <a href="/wiki/Emasculated" class="mw-redirect" title="Emasculated">emasculation</a> as an autonomous creative individual. This blow is symbolized by his chest wound, inflicted at the moment he is forced to invent things for the purposes of others, instead of just himself. To Genter, Stark\'s transformation into Iron Man represents Stark\'s effort to reclaim his autonomy, and thus his manhood. The character\'s pursuit of women in bed or in battle, writes Genter, represents another aspect of this effort. The pattern finds parallels in other works of 1960s popular fiction by authors such as "<a href="/wiki/Ian_Fleming" title="Ian Fleming">Ian Fleming</a> (creator of <a href="/wiki/James_Bond" title="James Bond">James Bond</a>), <a href="/wiki/Mickey_Spillane" title="Mickey Spillane">Mickey Spillane</a> (<a href="/wiki/Mike_Hammer" title="Mike Hammer">Mike Hammer</a>), and <a href="/wiki/Norman_Mailer" title="Norman Mailer">Norman Mailer</a>, who made unregulated sexuality a form of authenticity."<sup id="cite_ref-JournalofPopCulture_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JournalofPopCulture-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>\n</p>\n<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Solo_series">Solo series</span></h3>\n<p>After issue #99 (March 1968), the <i>Tales of Suspense</i> series was renamed <i>Captain America</i>. An Iron Man story appeared in the <a href="/wiki/One-shot_(comics)" title="One-shot (comics)">one-shot</a> comic <i><a href="/wiki/Iron_Man_and_Sub-Mariner" title="Iron Man and Sub-Mariner">Iron Man and Sub-Mariner</a></i> (April 1968), before the "Golden Avenger"<sup id="cite_ref-iron_man_spotlight_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-iron_man_spotlight-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> made his solo debut with <i><a href="/wiki/Iron_Man_(comic_book)" title="Iron Man (comic book)">Iron Man</a></i> #1 (May 1968).<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The series\' <a href="/wiki/Indicia_(publishing)" title="Indicia (publishing)">indicia</a> gives its <a href="/wiki/Copyright" title="Copyright">copyright</a> title <i>Iron Man,</i> while the <a href="/wiki/Trademark" title="Trademark">trademarked</a> cover logo of most issues is <i>The Invincible Iron Man.</i>\n</p><p>This initial series ended with issue #332 (Sept. 1996). <a href="/wiki/Jim_Lee" title="Jim Lee">Jim Lee</a>, <a href="/wiki/Scott_Lobdell" title="Scott Lobdell">Scott Lobdell</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Jeph_Loeb" title="Jeph Loeb">Jeph Loeb</a> authored a second volume of the series which was <a href="/wiki/Penciler" class="mw-redirect" title="Penciler">drawn</a> primarily by <a href="/wiki/Whilce_Portacio" title="Whilce Portacio">Whilce Portacio</a> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Ryan_Benjamin_(comics)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Ryan Benjamin (comics) (page does not exist)">Ryan Benjamin</a>. This volume took place in a <a href="/wiki/Parallel_universe_(fiction)" class="mw-redirect" title="Parallel universe (fiction)">parallel universe</a><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> and ran 13 issues (Nov. 1996 \xe2\x80\x93 Nov. 1997).<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Volume 3, whose first 25 issues were written by <a href="/wiki/Kurt_Busiek" title="Kurt Busiek">Kurt Busiek</a><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> and then by Busiek and <a href="/wiki/Roger_Stern" title="Roger Stern">Roger Stern</a>, ran 89 issues (Feb. 1998 \xe2\x80\x93 Dec. 2004). Later writers included <a href="/wiki/Joe_Quesada" title="Joe Quesada">Joe Quesada</a>, <a href="/wiki/Frank_Tieri_(comics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Frank Tieri (comics)">Frank Tieri</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mike_Grell" title="Mike Grell">Mike Grell</a>, and <a href="/wiki/John_Jackson_Miller" title="John Jackson Miller">John Jackson Miller</a>. Issue #41 (June 2001) was additionally numbered #386, reflecting the start of dual numbering starting from the premiere issue of volume one in 1968. The final issue was dual-numbered as #434.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The next Iron Man series, <i>Iron Man</i> vol. 4, debuted in early 2005 with the <a href="/wiki/Warren_Ellis" title="Warren Ellis">Warren Ellis</a>-written storyline "<a href="/wiki/Extremis_(comics)" class="mw-redirect" title="Extremis (comics)">Extremis</a>", with artist <a href="/wiki/Adi_Granov" title="Adi Granov">Adi Granov</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> It ran 35 issues (Jan. 2005 \xe2\x80\x93 Jan. 2009), with the cover logo simply <i>Iron Man</i> beginning with issue #13, and <i>Iron Man: Director of <a href="/wiki/S.H.I.E.L.D." title="S.H.I.E.L.D.">S.H.I.E.L.D.</a></i>, beginning issue #15. On the final three issues, the cover logo was overwritten by "War Machine, Weapon of S.H.I.E.L.D.",<sup id="cite_ref-vol4_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vol4-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> which led to the launch of a <i><a href="/wiki/War_Machine" title="War Machine">War Machine</a></i> ongoing series.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>\n</p><p><i>The Invincible Iron Man</i> vol. 1, by writer <a href="/wiki/Matt_Fraction" title="Matt Fraction">Matt Fraction</a> and artist <a href="/wiki/Salvador_Larroca" title="Salvador Larroca">Salvador Larroca</a>, began with a premiere issue cover-dated July 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-fraction_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fraction-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> For a seven-month overlap, Marvel published both volume four and volume five simultaneously.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> This <i>Invincible</i> volume jumped its numbering of issues from #33 to #500, cover dated March 2011, to reflect the start from the premiere issue of volume one in 1968.\n</p><p>After the conclusion of <i>The Invincible Iron Man</i> a new Iron Man series was started as a part of <a href="/wiki/Marvel_Now!" class="mw-redirect" title="Marvel Now!">Marvel Now!</a>. Written by <a href="/wiki/Kieron_Gillen" title="Kieron Gillen">Kieron Gillen</a> and illustrated by <a href="/wiki/Greg_Land" title="Greg Land">Greg Land</a>, it began with issue #1 in November 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>\n</p><p>Many Iron Man <a href="/wiki/Annual_publication" title="Annual publication">annuals</a>, <a href="/wiki/Miniseries" title="Miniseries">miniseries</a>, and <a href="/wiki/One-shot_(comics)" title="One-shot (comics)">one-shot</a> titles have been published through the years, such as <i>Age of Innocence: The Rebirth of Iron Man</i> (Feb. 1996), <i>Iron Man: The Iron Age</i> #1\xe2\x80\x932 (Aug.&#8211;Sept. 1998), <i>Iron Man: Bad Blood</i> #1\xe2\x80\x934 (Sept.&#8211;Dec. 2000), <i>Iron Man House of M</i> #1\xe2\x80\x933 (Sept.&#8211;Nov. 2005), <i>Fantastic Four / Iron Man: Big in Japan</i> #1\xe2\x80\x934 (Dec. 2005&#8211;March 2006), <i>Iron Man: The Inevitable</i> #1\xe2\x80\x936 (Feb.&#8211;July 2006), <i>Iron Man / Captain America: Casualties of War</i> (Feb. 2007), <i>Iron Man: Hypervelocity</i> #1\xe2\x80\x936 (March&#8211;Aug. 2007), <i>Iron Man: Enter the Mandarin</i> #1\xe2\x80\x936 (Nov. 2007&#8211;April 2008), and <i>Iron Man: Legacy of Doom</i> (June&#8211;Sept. 2008). Publications have included such spin-offs as the one-shot <i>Iron Man 2020</i> (June 1994), featuring a different Iron Man in the future, and the <a href="/wiki/Animated_TV_series" class="mw-redirect" title="Animated TV series">animated TV series</a> adaptations <i>Marvel Action Hour, Featuring Iron Man</i> #1\xe2\x80\x938 (Nov. 1994&#8211;June 1995) and <i>Marvel Adventures Iron Man</i> #1\xe2\x80\x9312 (July 2007&#8211;June 2008).<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>\n</p>\n<h2><span class="mw-headline"
